<p><span typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg/50px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="42" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg/75px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg/100px-Royal_Coat_of_Arms_of_the_United_Kingdom_%28HM_Government%29_%282022%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1516" data-file-height="1265" /></span></span>
</p>
<span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/List_of_coats_of_arms_of_Prime_Ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="List of coats of arms of Prime Ministers of the United Kingdom"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Robert_Peel_Arms.svg/30px-Robert_Peel_Arms.svg.png" decoding="async" width="30" height="36" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Robert_Peel_Arms.svg/45px-Robert_Peel_Arms.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Robert_Peel_Arms.svg/60px-Robert_Peel_Arms.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="317" data-file-height="381" /></a></span></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Robert_Peel_sidebar" title="Template:Robert Peel sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/w/index.php?title=Template_talk:Robert_Peel_sidebar&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Template talk:Robert Peel sidebar (page does not exist)"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Robert_Peel_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Sir Robert Peel, 2nd Baronet</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">FRS</a></span> (5 February 1788 – 2 July 1850), was a British <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a> statesman who served twice as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> (1834–1835 and 1841–1846), simultaneously serving as <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Chancellor of the Exchequer">Chancellor of the Exchequer</a> (1834–1835), and twice as <a href="/wiki/Home_Secretary" title="Home Secretary">Home Secretary</a> (1822–1827 and 1828–1830). He is regarded as the father of modern <a href="/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Law enforcement in the United Kingdom">British policing</a>, owing to his founding of the <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_Police_Service" class="mw-redirect" title="Metropolitan Police Service">Metropolitan Police Service</a>. Peel was one of the founders of the modern <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative Party</a>.
</p><p>The son of a wealthy textile manufacturer and politician, Peel was the first prime minister from an industrial business background. He earned a <a href="/wiki/Double_first" class="mw-redirect" title="Double first">double first</a> in classics and mathematics from <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church, Oxford</a>. He entered the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> in 1809, and became a rising star in the Tory Party. Peel entered <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Cabinet of the United Kingdom">the Cabinet</a> as Home Secretary (1822–1827), where he reformed and liberalised the criminal law and created the modern police force, leading to a new type of officer known in tribute to him as "bobbies" and "peelers". After a brief period out of office he returned as Home Secretary under his political mentor the <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Duke of Wellington (1828–1830)</a>, also serving as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Commons" title="Leader of the House of Commons">Leader of the House of Commons</a>. Initially, a supporter of <a href="/wiki/Catholic_emancipation" title="Catholic emancipation">continued legal discrimination against Catholics</a>, Peel reversed himself and supported the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Relief_Act_1829" title="Roman Catholic Relief Act 1829">Roman Catholic Relief Act 1829</a> and the 1828 repeal of the <a href="/wiki/Test_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Test Act">Test Act</a>, claiming that "though emancipation was a great danger, civil strife was a greater danger".<sup id="cite_ref-DNB_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After being in Opposition from 1830 to 1834, he became Prime Minister in November 1834. Peel issued the <a href="/wiki/Tamworth_Manifesto" title="Tamworth Manifesto">Tamworth Manifesto</a> (December 1834), laying down the principles upon which the modern British Conservative Party is based. His <a href="/wiki/First_Peel_ministry" title="First Peel ministry">first ministry</a> was a minority government, dependent on Whig support and with Peel serving as his own <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Chancellor of the Exchequer">Chancellor of the Exchequer</a>. After only four months, his government collapsed and he served as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(United_Kingdom)" title="Leader of the Opposition (United Kingdom)">Leader of the Opposition</a> during <a href="/wiki/Second_Melbourne_ministry" title="Second Melbourne ministry">Melbourne's second government</a> (1835–1841). Peel became Prime Minister again after the <a href="/wiki/1841_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1841 United Kingdom general election">1841 general election</a>. His <a href="/wiki/Second_Peel_ministry" title="Second Peel ministry">second government</a> ruled for five years. He cut tariffs to stimulate trade, replacing the lost revenue with a 3% <a href="/wiki/Income_tax" title="Income tax">income tax</a>. He played a central role in making <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a> a reality and set up a modern banking system. His government's major legislation included the <a href="/wiki/Mines_and_Collieries_Act_1842" title="Mines and Collieries Act 1842">Mines and Collieries Act 1842</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Income_Tax_Act_1842" title="Income Tax Act 1842">Income Tax Act 1842</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Factory_Acts#Factories_Act_1844_(&#39;Graham&#39;s_Factory_Act&#39;)" title="Factory Acts">Factories Act 1844</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Railway_Regulation_Act_1844" title="Railway Regulation Act 1844">Railway Regulation Act 1844</a>. Peel's government was weakened by anti-Catholic sentiment following the controversial increase in the <a href="/wiki/Maynooth_Grant" title="Maynooth Grant">Maynooth Grant</a> of 1845. After the outbreak of the <a href="/wiki/Great_Famine_(Ireland)" title="Great Famine (Ireland)">Great Irish Famine</a>, his decision to join with Whigs and <a href="/wiki/Radicals_(UK)" title="Radicals (UK)">Radicals</a> to repeal the <a href="/wiki/Corn_Laws" title="Corn Laws">Corn Laws</a> led to his resignation as Prime Minister in 1846. Peel remained an influential MP and leader of the <a href="/wiki/Peelite" title="Peelite">Peelite</a> faction until his death in 1850.
</p><p>Peel often started from a traditional Tory position in opposition to a measure, then reversed his stance and became the leader in supporting liberal legislation. This happened with the Test Act, <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Emancipation" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic Emancipation">Catholic Emancipation</a>, the Reform Act, income tax and, most notably, the repeal of the Corn Laws. Historian <a href="/wiki/A._J._P._Taylor" title="A. J. P. Taylor">A. J. P. Taylor</a> wrote: "Peel was in the first rank of 19th-century statesmen. He carried Catholic Emancipation; he repealed the Corn Laws; he created the modern Conservative Party on the ruins of the <a href="/wiki/Tory_(British_political_party)" class="mw-redirect" title="Tory (British political party)">old Toryism</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Peel was born at Chamber Hall, <a href="/wiki/Bury,_Greater_Manchester" title="Bury, Greater Manchester">Bury</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lancashire" title="Lancashire">Lancashire</a>, to the industrialist and parliamentarian <a href="/wiki/Sir_Robert_Peel,_1st_Baronet" title="Sir Robert Peel, 1st Baronet">Sir Robert Peel, 1st Baronet</a>, and his wife Ellen Yates. His father was one of the richest textile manufacturers of the early <a href="/wiki/Industrial_Revolution" title="Industrial Revolution">Industrial Revolution</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> The family moved from Lancashire to <a href="/wiki/Drayton_Manor" title="Drayton Manor">Drayton Manor</a> near <a href="/wiki/Tamworth,_Staffordshire" title="Tamworth, Staffordshire">Tamworth, Staffordshire</a>; the manor house has since been demolished, and the site occupied by <a href="/wiki/Drayton_Manor_Theme_Park" class="mw-redirect" title="Drayton Manor Theme Park">Drayton Manor Theme Park</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Peel received his early education from a clergyman tutor in Bury and at a clergyman's local school in Tamworth.<sup id="cite_ref-DNB_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> He may also have attended <a href="/wiki/Bury_Grammar_School" title="Bury Grammar School">Bury Grammar School</a> or <a href="/wiki/Hipperholme_Grammar_School" title="Hipperholme Grammar School">Hipperholme Grammar School</a>, though evidence for either is anecdotal rather than textual.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> He started at <a href="/wiki/Harrow_School" title="Harrow School">Harrow School</a> in February 1800.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>At Harrow, he was a contemporary of <a href="/wiki/Lord_Byron" title="Lord Byron">Lord Byron</a>, who recalled of Peel that "we were on good terms" and that "I was always in scrapes, and he never".<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> On Harrow's Speech Day in 1804, Peel and Byron acted part of <a href="/wiki/Virgil" title="Virgil">Virgil</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Aeneid" title="Aeneid">Aeneid</a></i>, Peel playing <a href="/wiki/Turnus" title="Turnus">Turnus</a> and Byron playing <a href="/wiki/Latinus" title="Latinus">Latinus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DNB_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tom_quad_Tom_tower_by_Pavel_Kliuiev.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Tom_quad_Tom_tower_by_Pavel_Kliuiev.jpg/170px-Tom_quad_Tom_tower_by_Pavel_Kliuiev.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="113" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Tom_quad_Tom_tower_by_Pavel_Kliuiev.jpg/255px-Tom_quad_Tom_tower_by_Pavel_Kliuiev.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Tom_quad_Tom_tower_by_Pavel_Kliuiev.jpg/340px-Tom_quad_Tom_tower_by_Pavel_Kliuiev.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5208" data-file-height="3476" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church, Oxford</a>, which Peel attended 1805–1808, graduating with a double first. He was later MP for the university, 1817–1829.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1805, Peel matriculated at <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church, Oxford</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> His tutor was <a href="/wiki/Charles_Lloyd_(bishop)" title="Charles Lloyd (bishop)">Charles Lloyd</a>, later <a href="/wiki/Regius_Professor_of_Divinity" title="Regius Professor of Divinity">Regius Professor of Divinity</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> on Peel's recommendation appointed <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Oxford" title="Bishop of Oxford">Bishop of Oxford</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> In 1808 Peel became the first Oxford student to take a double first in Classics and Mathematics.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Peel was a law student at <a href="/wiki/Lincoln%27s_Inn" title="Lincoln&#39;s Inn">Lincoln's Inn</a> in 1809.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He also held military commissions as a captain in the Manchester Regiment of <a href="/wiki/Militia" title="Militia">Militia</a> in 1808,<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> and later as lieutenant in the <a href="/wiki/Staffordshire_Yeomanry" title="Staffordshire Yeomanry">Staffordshire Yeomanry Cavalry</a> in 1820.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Early_political_career:_1809.E2.80.931822"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career:_1809–1822">Early political career: 1809–1822</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early political career: 1809–1822">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Member_of_Parliament">Member of Parliament</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Member of Parliament">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Peel entered politics in 1809 at the age of 21, as MP for the Irish <a href="/wiki/Rotten_borough" class="mw-redirect" title="Rotten borough">rotten borough</a> of <a href="/wiki/Cashel_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Cashel (UK Parliament constituency)">Cashel</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Tipperary" title="County Tipperary">Tipperary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> With a scant 24 electors on the rolls, he was elected unopposed. His sponsor for the election (besides his father) was the <a href="/wiki/Chief_Secretary_for_Ireland" title="Chief Secretary for Ireland">Chief Secretary for Ireland</a>, Sir <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Arthur Wellesley</a>, the future Duke of Wellington, with whom Peel's political career would be entwined for the next 25 years. Peel made his <a href="/wiki/Maiden_speech" title="Maiden speech">maiden speech</a> at the start of the 1810 session, when he was chosen by Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Spencer_Perceval" title="Spencer Perceval">Spencer Perceval</a> to second the reply to the <a href="/wiki/Speech_from_the_Throne" class="mw-redirect" title="Speech from the Throne">king's speech</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> His speech was a sensation, famously described by the <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_House_of_Commons_(United_Kingdom)" title="Speaker of the House of Commons (United Kingdom)">Speaker</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Abbot,_1st_Baron_Colchester" title="Charles Abbot, 1st Baron Colchester">Charles Abbot</a>, as "the best first speech since that of <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger" title="William Pitt the Younger">William Pitt</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Peel changed constituency twice, becoming one of the two Members for <a href="/wiki/Chippenham_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Chippenham (UK Parliament constituency)">Chippenham</a> in 1812, and then one of those for <a href="/wiki/Oxford_University_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Oxford University (UK Parliament constituency)">Oxford University</a> in 1817.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Junior_minister">Junior minister</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Junior minister">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1810, Peel was appointed an <a href="/wiki/Under-Secretary_of_State_for_War_and_the_Colonies" title="Under-Secretary of State for War and the Colonies">Under-Secretary of State for War and the Colonies</a>; his Secretary of State was <a href="/wiki/Robert_Jenkinson,_2nd_Earl_of_Liverpool" title="Robert Jenkinson, 2nd Earl of Liverpool">Lord Liverpool</a>. When Lord Liverpool formed a government in 1812, Peel was appointed <a href="/wiki/Chief_Secretary_for_Ireland" title="Chief Secretary for Ireland">Chief Secretary for Ireland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DNB_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> The Peace Preservation Act of 1814 authorised the Lord Lieutenant of Ireland to appoint additional magistrates in a county in a state of disturbance, who were authorised to appoint paid special constables (later called "peelers"<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>). Peel thus laid the basis for the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Irish_Constabulary" title="Royal Irish Constabulary">Royal Irish Constabulary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Peel was firmly opposed to <a href="/wiki/Catholic_emancipation" title="Catholic emancipation">Catholic emancipation</a>, believing that Catholics could not be admitted to Parliament as they refused to swear the <a href="/wiki/Oath_of_Allegiance_(United_Kingdom)" title="Oath of Allegiance (United Kingdom)">Oath of Allegiance</a> to the Crown.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> In May 1817, Peel delivered the closing speech in opposition to <a href="/wiki/Henry_Grattan" title="Henry Grattan">Henry Grattan</a>'s Catholic emancipation bill; the bill was defeated by 245 votes to 221.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Peel resigned as Chief Secretary and left Ireland in August 1818.<sup id="cite_ref-DNB_1-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1819, the House of Commons appointed a Select Committee, the <a href="/wiki/Bullion_Committee" title="Bullion Committee">Bullion Committee</a>, charged with stabilising British finances after the end of the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a>, and Peel was chosen as its chairman.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Peel%27s_Bill" title="Peel&#39;s Bill">Peel's Bill</a> planned to return British currency to the <a href="/wiki/Gold_standard" title="Gold standard">gold standard</a>, reversing the <a href="/wiki/Bank_Restriction_Act_1797" title="Bank Restriction Act 1797">Bank Restriction Act 1797</a>, within four years (it was actually accomplished by 1821).<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Home_Secretary:_1822.E2.80.931830"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Home_Secretary:_1822–1830">Home Secretary: 1822–1830</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Home Secretary: 1822–1830">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Victorian_morality#Crime_and_police" title="Victorian morality">Victorian morality §&#160;Crime and police</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Duke_of_Wellington_and_Sir_Robert_Peel_1844.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/The_Duke_of_Wellington_and_Sir_Robert_Peel_1844.jpg/170px-The_Duke_of_Wellington_and_Sir_Robert_Peel_1844.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="265" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/The_Duke_of_Wellington_and_Sir_Robert_Peel_1844.jpg/255px-The_Duke_of_Wellington_and_Sir_Robert_Peel_1844.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/The_Duke_of_Wellington_and_Sir_Robert_Peel_1844.jpg/340px-The_Duke_of_Wellington_and_Sir_Robert_Peel_1844.jpg 2x" data-file-width="857" data-file-height="1334" /></a><figcaption>The Duke of Wellington, Prime Minister 1828–1830, with Peel</figcaption></figure>
<p>Peel was considered one of the rising stars of the Tory party, first entering the cabinet in 1822 as <a href="/wiki/Home_Secretary" title="Home Secretary">Home Secretary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> As Home Secretary, he introduced a large number of important reforms to British criminal law and transformed the legislature, the justice system and prisons.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Reforms_and_policies">Reforms and policies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Reforms and policies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In one of his policies, he reduced the number of crimes punishable by death, and simplified the law by repealing a large number of criminal statutes and consolidating their provisions into what are known as <a href="/wiki/Peel%27s_Acts" title="Peel&#39;s Acts">Peel's Acts</a>. He reformed the gaol system, introducing payment for gaolers and education for the inmates in the <a href="/wiki/Gaols_Act_1823" title="Gaols Act 1823">Gaols Act 1823</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1827 the Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Robert_Jenkinson,_2nd_Earl_of_Liverpool" title="Robert Jenkinson, 2nd Earl of Liverpool">Lord Liverpool</a> became incapacitated and was replaced by <a href="/wiki/George_Canning" title="George Canning">George Canning</a>. Peel resigned as home secretary.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Canning favoured <a href="/wiki/Catholic_emancipation" title="Catholic emancipation">Catholic emancipation</a>, while Peel had been one of its most outspoken opponents (earning the nickname "Orange Peel", with Orange the colour of the anti-Catholic <a href="/wiki/Orange_Order" title="Orange Order">Orange Order</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> George Canning himself died less than four months later and, after the brief premiership of <a href="/wiki/Frederick_John_Robinson,_1st_Viscount_Goderich" class="mw-redirect" title="Frederick John Robinson, 1st Viscount Goderich">Lord Goderich</a>, Peel returned to the post of Home Secretary under the premiership of his long-time ally the <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Duke of Wellington</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> During this time he was widely perceived as the number-two in the Tory Party, after Wellington himself.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Test_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Test Act">Test and Corporation Acts</a> required many officials to be communicants in the Anglican Church and penalised both nonconformists and Catholics. They were no longer enforced but were a matter of humiliation. Peel at first opposed the repeal, but reversed himself and led the repeal on behalf of the government, after consultation with Anglican Church leaders.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Sacramental_Test_Act_1828" title="Sacramental Test Act 1828">Sacramental Test Act 1828</a> passed into law in May 1828. In future religious issues he made it a point to consult with church leaders from the major denominations.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/1828_Clare_by-election" title="1828 Clare by-election">1828 Clare by-election</a> returned the Catholic Irish nationalist leader <a href="/wiki/Daniel_O%27Connell" title="Daniel O&#39;Connell">Daniel O'Connell</a>. By autumn 1828, the Chief Secretary for Ireland was alarmed by the extent of civil disorder and the prospect of a rebellion<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> if O'Connell were barred from Parliament. Wellington and Peel now conceded the necessity of Catholic emancipation, Peel writing to Wellington that "though emancipation was a great danger, civil strife was a greater danger".<sup id="cite_ref-DNB_1-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Peel drew up the Catholic Relief bill. He felt compelled to stand for re-election to his seat in Oxford, as he was representing the graduates of Oxford University (many of whom were Anglican clergymen), and had previously stood on a platform of opposition to Catholic Emancipation.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Peel lost his seat in a by-election in February 1829, but soon found another, moving to a <a href="/wiki/Rotten_borough" class="mw-redirect" title="Rotten borough">rotten borough</a>, <a href="/wiki/Westbury_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Westbury (UK Parliament constituency)">Westbury</a>, retaining his Cabinet position.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> He stood for <a href="/wiki/Tamworth_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Tamworth (UK Parliament constituency)">Tamworth</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1830_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1830 United Kingdom general election">general election of 1830</a>, representing Tamworth until his death.
</p><p>Peel guided the Catholic Relief (Emancipation) bill through the House of Commons, Wellington through the House of Lords. With many <a href="/wiki/Ultra-Tories" title="Ultra-Tories">Ultra-Tories</a> vehemently opposed to emancipation, the bill could pass only with Whig support.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Wellington threatened to resign if <a href="/wiki/George_IV" title="George IV">King George IV</a> did not give Royal assent;<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> the King finally relented. The <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Relief_Act_1829" title="Roman Catholic Relief Act 1829">Roman Catholic Relief Act 1829</a> passed into law in April 1829. Peel's U-turn cost him the trust of many Tories:<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> according to <a href="/wiki/Norman_Gash" title="Norman Gash">Norman Gash</a>, Peel had been "the idolized champion of the Protestant party; that party now regarded him as an outcast".<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Burking_Poor_Old_Mrs_Constitution._Wellcome_L0019663.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Burking_Poor_Old_Mrs_Constitution._Wellcome_L0019663.jpg/220px-Burking_Poor_Old_Mrs_Constitution._Wellcome_L0019663.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="158" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Burking_Poor_Old_Mrs_Constitution._Wellcome_L0019663.jpg/330px-Burking_Poor_Old_Mrs_Constitution._Wellcome_L0019663.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Burking_Poor_Old_Mrs_Constitution._Wellcome_L0019663.jpg/440px-Burking_Poor_Old_Mrs_Constitution._Wellcome_L0019663.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1668" data-file-height="1198" /></a><figcaption>This satirical 1829 cartoon by <a href="/wiki/William_Heath_(artist)" title="William Heath (artist)">William Heath</a> depicted <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">the Duke of Wellington</a> and Peel in the roles of the body-snatchers <a href="/wiki/Burke_and_Hare_murders" title="Burke and Hare murders">Burke and Hare</a> suffocating Mrs Docherty for sale to Dr. Knox; representing the extinguishing by Wellington and Peel of the 141-year-old Constitution of 1688 by <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Emancipation" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic Emancipation">Catholic Emancipation</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Founding_the_Metropolitan_Police">Founding the Metropolitan Police</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Founding the Metropolitan Police">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>It was in 1829 that Peel established the <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_Police_Force" class="mw-redirect" title="Metropolitan Police Force">Metropolitan Police Force</a> for London based at <a href="/wiki/Scotland_Yard" title="Scotland Yard">Scotland Yard</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> The 1,000 constables employed were affectionately nicknamed 'bobbies' or, somewhat less affectionately, 'peelers'. Although unpopular at first, they proved very successful in cutting crime in London,<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> and, by 1857, all cities in Britain were obliged to form their own police forces.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Known as the father of modern policing, Peel is thought to have contributed to the Metropolitan Police's first set of "Instructions to Police Officers", emphasising the importance of its civilian nature and policing by consent. However, what are now commonly known as the <a href="/wiki/Peelian_Principles" class="mw-redirect" title="Peelian Principles">Peelian Principles</a> were not written by him but were instead produced by <a href="/w/index.php?title=Charles_Reith&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Charles Reith (page does not exist)">Charles Reith</a> in his 1948 book, <i>A Short History of the British Police</i>, as a nine-point summary of the 1829 "Instructions".<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tory_opposition">Tory opposition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Tory opposition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The middle and working classes in England at that time, however, were clamouring for reform, and Catholic Emancipation was only one of the ideas in the air.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The Tory ministry refused to bend on other issues and were swept out of office in 1830 in favour of the <a href="/wiki/British_Whig_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="British Whig Party">Whigs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The following few years were extremely turbulent, but eventually <a href="/wiki/Reform_Act_1832" title="Reform Act 1832">enough reforms</a> were passed that <a href="/wiki/William_IV" title="William IV">King William IV</a> felt confident enough to invite the Tories to form a ministry again in succession to those of <a href="/wiki/Charles_Grey,_2nd_Earl_Grey" title="Charles Grey, 2nd Earl Grey">Lord Grey</a> and <a href="/wiki/William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne" title="William Lamb, 2nd Viscount Melbourne">Lord Melbourne</a> in December 1834.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Peel was selected as prime minister but was in Italy at the time, so Wellington acted as a caretaker for three weeks until Peel's return.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="First_term_as_Prime_Minister:_1834.E2.80.931835"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="First_term_as_Prime_Minister:_1834–1835">First term as Prime Minister: 1834–1835<span class="anchor" id="First_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: First term as Prime Minister: 1834–1835">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Government">Government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/First_Peel_ministry" title="First Peel ministry">First Peel ministry</a></div>
<p>Following the resignation of former Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Charles_Grey,_2nd_Earl_Grey" title="Charles Grey, 2nd Earl Grey">Charles Grey</a>, because an issue regarding Ireland's conciliatory reform and at the invitation of King <a href="/wiki/William_IV" title="William IV">William IV</a>, Peel became prime minister on June of 1834. Peel formed his own government, though it was a Tory government that was a minority government and depended on Whig goodwill for its continued existence. Parliament was dissolved in December 1834 and a general election was called. Voting took place in January and February 1835, and Peel's supporters gained around 100 seats, but this was not enough to give them a majority.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tamworth_Manifesto">Tamworth Manifesto</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Tamworth Manifesto">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>As his statement of policy at the general election of January 1835, Peel issued the <a href="/wiki/Tamworth_Manifesto" title="Tamworth Manifesto">Tamworth Manifesto</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> This document was the basis on which the modern <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative Party</a> was founded. In it, Peel pledged that the Conservatives would endorse <em>modest</em> reform such as reforms concerning economic and financial affairs, free trade and factory worker's rights.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Whigs formed a compact with <a href="/wiki/Daniel_O%27Connell" title="Daniel O&#39;Connell">Daniel O'Connell</a>'s Irish Radical members to repeatedly defeat the government on various bills.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Eventually, after only about 100 days in government, Peel's ministry resigned out of frustration and the Whigs under Lord Melbourne returned to power.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> The only real achievement of Peel's first administration was a commission to review the governance of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a>. This ecclesiastical commission was the forerunner of the <a href="/wiki/Church_Commissioners" title="Church Commissioners">Church Commissioners</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Return_to_opposition">Return to opposition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Return to opposition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In May 1839 he was offered another chance to form a government, this time by the new monarch, <a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen Victoria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> However, this too would have been a minority government, and Peel felt he needed a further sign of confidence from his Queen. Lord Melbourne had been Victoria's confidant since her accession in 1837, and many of the higher posts in Victoria's household were held by the wives and female relatives of Whigs;<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> there was some feeling that Victoria had allowed herself to be too closely associated with the Whig party. Peel, therefore, asked that some of this entourage be dismissed and replaced with their Conservative counterparts, provoking the so-called <a href="/wiki/Bedchamber_Crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Bedchamber Crisis">Bedchamber Crisis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Victoria refused to change her household, and despite pleadings from the Duke of Wellington, relied on assurances of support from Whig leaders. Peel refused to form a government, and the Whigs returned to power.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Second_term_as_Prime_Minister:_1841.E2.80.931846"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_term_as_Prime_Minister:_1841–1846">Second term as Prime Minister: 1841–1846<span class="anchor" id="Second_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Second term as Prime Minister: 1841–1846">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Appointment">Appointment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Appointment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Second_Peel_ministry" title="Second Peel ministry">Second Peel ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Second_Peel_Ministry_1844_engraving.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Second_Peel_Ministry_1844_engraving.jpg/220px-Second_Peel_Ministry_1844_engraving.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="309" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Second_Peel_Ministry_1844_engraving.jpg/330px-Second_Peel_Ministry_1844_engraving.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/12/Second_Peel_Ministry_1844_engraving.jpg/440px-Second_Peel_Ministry_1844_engraving.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1334" data-file-height="1871" /></a><figcaption>Engraving showing the members of Sir Robert Peel's government in 1844</figcaption></figure>
<p>By 1839, the Whig government under <a href="/wiki/William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne" title="William Lamb, 2nd Viscount Melbourne">William Lamb, 2nd Viscount Melbourne</a>, whose government and party had both suffered and faced many calamitous defeats in the House of Commons, although Melbourne who had still retained the firm and unwavering support of the young <a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen</a>. But unfortunately for him, a vote of no confidence was set in motion by Peel himself and by fellow Conservative MP <a href="/wiki/John_Stuart-Wortley,_2nd_Baron_Wharncliffe" title="John Stuart-Wortley, 2nd Baron Wharncliffe">John Stuart-Wortley, 2nd Baron Wharncliffe</a> against the government. The Whig government first lost the budget vote by 36 votes and then the vote of no confidence by one vote, although due to precedent Melbourne should have resigned that same year, but his cabinet decided to seek a dissolution and though Melbourne opposed it, but later accepting his ministers' wishes, convinced the Queen to dissolve <a href="/wiki/UK_Parliament" class="mw-redirect" title="UK Parliament">Parliament</a>. In 1841, a general election was called by the incumbent Whig government and resulted in a major <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party" title="Conservative Party">Conservative Party</a> victory under Peel's leadership, who won a majority of around 367 seats in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons" title="House of Commons">House of Commons</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1841_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1841 United Kingdom general election">1841 general election</a> defeating the <a href="/wiki/Whigs_(British_political_party)" title="Whigs (British political party)">Whigs</a> under Melbourne, who only won 273 seats, leading to a Whig minority in Parliament. On August of 1842, Melbourne reluctantly resigned and Peel became Prime Minister for the second time, at the invitation of the Queen. And this led to the formation of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Peel_ministry" title="Second Peel ministry">Second Peel ministry</a> which would govern and dominate the legislature in Britain for the next five years.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_and_financial_reforms">Economic and financial reforms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Economic and financial reforms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Peel finally had a chance to head a majority government following the election of July 1841.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Peel came to office during an economic recession which had seen a slump in world trade and a budget deficit of £7.5&#160;million run up by the <a href="/wiki/Whig_(British_political_faction)" class="mw-redirect" title="Whig (British political faction)">Whigs</a>. Confidence in banks and businesses was low, and a trade deficit existed.
</p><p>To raise revenue Peel's 1842 budget saw the re-introduction of the <a href="/wiki/Taxation_in_the_United_Kingdom#History" title="Taxation in the United Kingdom">income tax</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> removed previously at the end of the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a>. The rate was 7d in the pound, or just under 3 per cent. The money raised was more than expected and allowed for the removal and reduction of over 1,200 tariffs on imports including the controversial <a href="/w/index.php?title=Sugar_duties&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Sugar duties (page does not exist)">sugar duties</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> It was also in the 1842 budget that the repeal of the <a href="/wiki/Corn_laws" class="mw-redirect" title="Corn laws">corn laws</a> was first proposed.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> It was defeated in a Commons vote by a margin of 4:1.
</p><p>
The <a href="/wiki/Economic_history" title="Economic history">economic historian</a> <a href="/wiki/Charles_Read_(historian)" title="Charles Read (historian)">Charles Read</a> has analysed Peel's economic policies as:</p><blockquote><p>(i) Fixing the value of British currency to a <a href="/wiki/Gold_standard" title="Gold standard">gold standard</a>, with the paper pound currency freely convertible to gold.
</p><p>(ii) A limited <a href="/wiki/Banknote" title="Banknote">banknote</a> supply based on a fixed relationship to the <a href="/wiki/Gold_reserves_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Gold reserves of the United Kingdom">gold reserve</a>.
</p><p>(iii) Free movement of bullion flows from 1819 and lower import <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariffs</a> on food and raw materials from 1842 (often loosely referred to as <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a>).
</p><p>
(iv) Control of <a href="/wiki/Interest_rate" title="Interest rate">interest rates</a> and a balanced <a href="/wiki/Budget" title="Budget">budget</a> in order to reduce the <a href="/wiki/Government_debt" title="Government debt">national debt</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Opium_War">The Opium War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: The Opium War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/First_Opium_War" title="First Opium War">First Opium War</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Signing_of_the_Treaty_of_Nanking.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/The_Signing_of_the_Treaty_of_Nanking.jpg/220px-The_Signing_of_the_Treaty_of_Nanking.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="83" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/The_Signing_of_the_Treaty_of_Nanking.jpg/330px-The_Signing_of_the_Treaty_of_Nanking.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/The_Signing_of_the_Treaty_of_Nanking.jpg/440px-The_Signing_of_the_Treaty_of_Nanking.jpg 2x" data-file-width="9686" data-file-height="3633" /></a><figcaption><b>The Signing of the Treaty of Nanking.</b> A <a href="/wiki/Oil_painting" title="Oil painting">Oil painting</a> depicting the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Nanking" title="Treaty of Nanking">Treaty of Nanking</a>, which officially ended the <a href="/wiki/First_Opium_War" title="First Opium War">First Opium War</a> between Britain and China.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1838, before Peel became prime minister, there have been growing tensions between the <a href="/wiki/British_East_India_Company" class="mw-redirect" title="British East India Company">British East India Company</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Chinese_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Chinese Empire">Chinese Empire</a> during the past years over the importation and trade of <a href="/wiki/Opium" title="Opium">opium</a>, which had been occurring for nearly a century. And when the trade of this particular product, due to it being smuggled unlawfully by merchants, it become a serious political and economic problem, was it was largely controversial. During the same year, opium became prohibited and made illegal by the Chinese imperial government under the <a href="/wiki/Daoguang_Emperor" title="Daoguang Emperor">Daoguang Emperor</a>. And as a result, the British government saw this as an offence against <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a> and against British merchant's rights so war began in 1839. In 1842, the time by when Peel became <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister</a>, the war still continued with numerous British military successes against the Chinese navy and when Peel continued the British interests in the war in China, linked to the opium commerce, was able to guide the war to a successful end by signing on 29 August 1842 the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Nanking" title="Treaty of Nanking">Treaty of Nanking</a> which effectively ended the <a href="/wiki/First_Opium_War" title="First Opium War">First Opium War</a> (1839–1842) between <a href="/wiki/Great_Britain" title="Great Britain">Great Britain</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Qing_dynasty" title="Qing dynasty">Qing dynasty</a> of China. It concluded with <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">China</a>'s disastrous military defeat, which led to the Chinese ceding of the island of <a href="/wiki/Hong_Kong" title="Hong Kong">Hong Kong</a> to the British and agreeing to more economic openness, which ultimately weakened the Qing regime and imperial influence during the following decades.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Factory_Act">Factory Act</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Factory Act">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Peel's promise of modest reform was held to, and the second most famous bill of this ministry, while "reforming" in 21st-century eyes, was in fact aimed at the reformers themselves, with their constituency among the new industrial rich. The <a href="/wiki/Factory_Act_1844" class="mw-redirect" title="Factory Act 1844">Factory Act 1844</a> acted more against these industrialists than it did against the traditional stronghold of the Conservatives, the <a href="/wiki/Landed_gentry" title="Landed gentry">landed gentry</a>, by restricting the number of hours that children and women could work in a factory and setting rudimentary safety standards for machinery.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> This was a continuation of his own father's work as an MP, as the elder Robert Peel was most noted for the reform of working conditions during the first part of the 19th century. Helping him was <a href="/wiki/Lord_Shaftesbury" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Shaftesbury">Lord Shaftesbury</a>, a British MP who also established the <a href="/wiki/Mines_and_Collieries_Act_1842" title="Mines and Collieries Act 1842">coal mines act</a>.
</p><p>In 1843 Peel was the target of a failed assassination attempt; a criminally insane Scottish woodturner named <a href="/wiki/Daniel_M%27Naghten" title="Daniel M&#39;Naghten">Daniel M'Naghten</a> stalked him for several days before, on 20 January, killing Peel's personal secretary <a href="/wiki/Edward_Drummond" title="Edward Drummond">Edward Drummond</a> thinking he was Peel,<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> which led to the formation of the controversial criminal defence of <a href="/wiki/Insanity_in_English_law" title="Insanity in English law">insanity</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Corn_Laws">Corn Laws</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Corn Laws">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Corn_Laws" title="Corn Laws">Corn Laws</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1815_Corn_Law,_An_Act_to_amend_the_Laws_now_in_force_for_regulating_the_Importation_of_Corn.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/1815_Corn_Law%2C_An_Act_to_amend_the_Laws_now_in_force_for_regulating_the_Importation_of_Corn.jpg/220px-1815_Corn_Law%2C_An_Act_to_amend_the_Laws_now_in_force_for_regulating_the_Importation_of_Corn.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="345" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/1815_Corn_Law%2C_An_Act_to_amend_the_Laws_now_in_force_for_regulating_the_Importation_of_Corn.jpg/330px-1815_Corn_Law%2C_An_Act_to_amend_the_Laws_now_in_force_for_regulating_the_Importation_of_Corn.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/1815_Corn_Law%2C_An_Act_to_amend_the_Laws_now_in_force_for_regulating_the_Importation_of_Corn.jpg/440px-1815_Corn_Law%2C_An_Act_to_amend_the_Laws_now_in_force_for_regulating_the_Importation_of_Corn.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1012" data-file-height="1586" /></a><figcaption>The 1815 Corn Laws, first introduced by the Tory government of  <a href="/wiki/Robert_Jenkinson,_2nd_Earl_of_Liverpool" title="Robert Jenkinson, 2nd Earl of Liverpool">Lord Liverpool</a>, the then Prime Minister. This law was made to amend the laws for regulating the importation of corn. This act was still in effect by the time Peel became prime minister himself in 1841.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The most notable act of Peel's second ministry, however, was the one that would bring it down.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Peel moved against the landholders by repealing the <a href="/wiki/Corn_Laws" title="Corn Laws">Corn Laws</a>, which supported agricultural revenues by restricting grain imports.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> This radical break with Conservative protectionism was triggered by the <a href="/wiki/Great_Irish_Famine" class="mw-redirect" title="Great Irish Famine">Great Irish Famine (1845–1849)</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Tory agriculturalists were sceptical of the extent of the problem,<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> and Peel reacted slowly to the famine, famously stating in October 1846 (already in opposition): "There is such a tendency to exaggeration and inaccuracy in Irish reports that delay in acting on them is always desirable".<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His own party failed to support the bill, but it passed with Whig and Radical support. On the <a href="/wiki/Reading_(legislature)" title="Reading (legislature)">third reading</a> of Peel's Bill of Repeal (Importation Act 1846) on 15 May, <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(United_Kingdom)" title="Member of Parliament (United Kingdom)">MPs</a> voted 327 votes to 229 (a majority of 98) to repeal the Corn Laws. On 25 June the Duke of Wellington persuaded the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a> to pass it. On that same night Peel's <a href="/wiki/Irish_Coercion_Bill" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish Coercion Bill">Irish Coercion Bill</a> was defeated in the Commons by 292 to 219 by "a combination of Whigs, <a href="/wiki/Radicals_(UK)" title="Radicals (UK)">Radicals</a>, and Tory protectionists".<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Following this, on 29 June 1846, Peel resigned as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Though he knew repealing the laws would mean the end of his ministry, Peel decided to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> It is possible that Peel merely used the Irish Famine as an excuse to repeal the Corn Laws as he had been an intellectual convert to free trade since the 1820s. Blake points out that if Peel had been convinced that total repeal was necessary to stave off the famine, he would have enacted a bill that brought about immediate temporary repeal, not permanent repeal over a three-year period of gradual tapering-off of duties.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
Peel's support for free trade could already be seen in his 1842 and 1845 budgets;<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> in late 1842 <a href="/wiki/Sir_James_Graham,_2nd_Baronet" title="Sir James Graham, 2nd Baronet">Graham</a> wrote to Peel that "the next change in the Corn Laws must be to an open trade" while arguing that the government should not tackle the issue.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Speaking to the cabinet in 1844, Peel argued that the choice was the maintenance of the 1842 Corn Law or total repeal.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> The historian <a href="/wiki/Boyd_Hilton" title="Boyd Hilton">Boyd Hilton</a> argued that Peel knew from 1844 he was going to be deposed as the Conservative leader. Many of his MPs had taken to voting against him, and the rupture within the party between liberals and paternalists, which had been so damaging in the 1820s but masked by the issue of parliamentary reform in the 1830s, was brought to the surface over the Corn Laws. Hilton's hypothesis is that Peel wished to be deposed on a liberal issue so that he might later lead a Peelite/Whig/Liberal alliance. Peel was magnanimous towards Irish famine and permitted quick settlements of disputes at frontiers in India and America ( <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Amritsar_(1846)" title="Treaty of Amritsar (1846)">Treaty of Amritsar (1846)</a> on 16 March 1846 and <a href="/wiki/Oregon_Treaty" title="Oregon Treaty">Oregon Treaty</a> on 15 June 1846) in order to repeal <a href="/wiki/Corn_Laws" title="Corn Laws">Corn Laws</a> on 29 June 1846.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> As an aside in reference to the repeal of the Corn Laws, Peel managed to keep minimum casualties of <a href="/wiki/Irish_Famine" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish Famine">Irish Famine</a> in its first year, Peel did make some moves to subsidise the purchase of food for the Irish, but this attempt was small and had little tangible effect. In the age of <a href="/wiki/Laissez-faire" title="Laissez-faire">laissez-faire</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> government taxes were small, and subsidies or direct economic interference was almost nonexistent. That subsidies were actually given was very much out of character for the political times; his successor, <a href="/wiki/John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell" title="John Russell, 1st Earl Russell">Lord John Russell</a>, received more criticism than Peel on Irish policy, the worst year being 1847,  despite all of Peel's efforts, his reform programs had little effect on the situation in Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Russell could not manage public distribution system during <a href="/wiki/Irish_Famine" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish Famine">Irish Famine</a> even though subsidized food from USA was made available in Ireland. The repeal of the Corn Laws became more political than humanitarian.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_career_and_death">Later career and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Later career and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Peel did, however, retain a hard core of supporters, known as <a href="/wiki/Peelite" title="Peelite">Peelites</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> and at one point in 1849 was actively courted by the Whig/Radical coalition. He continued to stand on his conservative principles, however, and refused. Nevertheless, he was influential on several important issues, including the furtherance of British free trade with the repeal of the <a href="/wiki/Navigation_Acts" title="Navigation Acts">Navigation Acts</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> Peel was a member of the committee which controlled the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_Library" title="House of Commons Library">House of Commons Library</a>, and on 16 April 1850 was responsible for passing the motion that controlled its scope and collection policy for the rest of the century.
</p><p>Peel was thrown from his horse while riding on <a href="/wiki/Constitution_Hill,_London" title="Constitution Hill, London">Constitution Hill</a> in London on 29 June 1850. The horse stumbled on top of him, and he died three days later on 2 July at the age of 62 due to a <a href="/wiki/Clavicle_fracture" title="Clavicle fracture">broken collarbone</a> rupturing his subclavian vessels.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His Peelite followers, led by <a href="/wiki/George_Hamilton-Gordon,_4th_Earl_of_Aberdeen" title="George Hamilton-Gordon, 4th Earl of Aberdeen">Lord Aberdeen</a> and <a href="/wiki/William_Ewart_Gladstone" title="William Ewart Gladstone">William Gladstone</a>, went on to fuse with the Whigs as the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family">Family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Julia,_Lady_Peel_-_Lawrence_1827.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Julia%2C_Lady_Peel_-_Lawrence_1827.jpg/220px-Julia%2C_Lady_Peel_-_Lawrence_1827.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="280" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Julia%2C_Lady_Peel_-_Lawrence_1827.jpg/330px-Julia%2C_Lady_Peel_-_Lawrence_1827.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Julia%2C_Lady_Peel_-_Lawrence_1827.jpg/440px-Julia%2C_Lady_Peel_-_Lawrence_1827.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4726" data-file-height="6013" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Thomas_Lawrence" title="Thomas Lawrence">Thomas Lawrence</a>'s portrait of his patron <i>Julia, Lady Peel</i> (1827), now in the <a href="/wiki/Frick_Collection" title="Frick Collection">Frick Collection</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>Peel became engaged to Julia Floyd (1795–1859) (daughter of General <a href="/wiki/Sir_John_Floyd,_1st_Baronet" title="Sir John Floyd, 1st Baronet">Sir John Floyd, 1st Baronet</a>, and his first wife Rebecca Darke) in March 1820 and was married on 8 June 1820.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> They had seven children:<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>Julia Peel (30 April 1821 – 14 August 1893). She married <a href="/wiki/George_Child_Villiers,_6th_Earl_of_Jersey" title="George Child Villiers, 6th Earl of Jersey">George Child Villiers, 6th Earl of Jersey</a>, on 12 July 1841. They had five children. She married her second husband, Charles Brandling, on 12 September 1865.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Sir_Robert_Peel,_3rd_Baronet" title="Sir Robert Peel, 3rd Baronet">Sir Robert Peel, 3rd Baronet</a> (4 May 1822 – 9 May 1895). He married <a href="/wiki/Emily,_Lady_Peel" title="Emily, Lady Peel">Lady Emily Hay</a> on 17 June 1856. They had five children.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Frederick_Peel" title="Frederick Peel">Sir Frederick Peel</a> (26 October 1823 – 6 June 1906). He married Elizabeth Shelley (niece of the poet <a href="/wiki/Percy_Bysshe_Shelley" title="Percy Bysshe Shelley">Percy Shelley</a> through his brother John: died 30 July 1865) on 12 August 1857. He was remarried to Janet Pleydell-Bouverie on 3 September 1879.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/William_Peel_(Royal_Navy_officer)" title="William Peel (Royal Navy officer)">Sir William Peel</a> (2 November 1824 – 27 April 1858).</li>
<li>John Floyd Peel (24 May 1827 – 21 April 1910). He married Annie Jenny in 1851.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Arthur_Peel,_1st_Viscount_Peel" title="Arthur Peel, 1st Viscount Peel">Arthur Peel, 1st Viscount Peel</a> (3 August 1829 – 24 October 1912). He married Adelaide Dugdale, daughter of William Stratford Dugdale and Harriet Ella Portman, on 14 August 1862. They had seven children. In 1895 he became Viscount Peel and was father of the first <a href="/wiki/Earl_Peel" title="Earl Peel">Earl Peel</a>.</li>
<li>Eliza Peel (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1832</span> – April 1883). She married Hon. Francis Stonor (son of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Stonor,_3rd_Baron_Camoys" title="Thomas Stonor, 3rd Baron Camoys">Thomas Stonor, 3rd Baron Camoys</a>) on 25 September 1855. They had four children.</li></ul>
<p>Julia, Lady Peel, died in 1859. Some of her direct descendants now reside in South Africa, the Australian states of <a href="/wiki/New_South_Wales" title="New South Wales">New South Wales</a>, <a href="/wiki/Queensland" title="Queensland">Queensland</a>, Victoria and <a href="/wiki/Tasmania" title="Tasmania">Tasmania</a>, and in various parts of the United States and Canada.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (June 2018)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Memory_and_recognition">Memory and recognition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Memory and recognition">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomas_Lawrence_-_Sir_Robert_Peel,_Bart_-_1957-15-2_-_Auckland_Art_Gallery.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Thomas_Lawrence_-_Sir_Robert_Peel%2C_Bart_-_1957-15-2_-_Auckland_Art_Gallery.jpg/170px-Thomas_Lawrence_-_Sir_Robert_Peel%2C_Bart_-_1957-15-2_-_Auckland_Art_Gallery.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="207" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Thomas_Lawrence_-_Sir_Robert_Peel%2C_Bart_-_1957-15-2_-_Auckland_Art_Gallery.jpg/255px-Thomas_Lawrence_-_Sir_Robert_Peel%2C_Bart_-_1957-15-2_-_Auckland_Art_Gallery.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Thomas_Lawrence_-_Sir_Robert_Peel%2C_Bart_-_1957-15-2_-_Auckland_Art_Gallery.jpg/340px-Thomas_Lawrence_-_Sir_Robert_Peel%2C_Bart_-_1957-15-2_-_Auckland_Art_Gallery.jpg 2x" data-file-width="632" data-file-height="768" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Robert Peel by <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Lawrence" title="Thomas Lawrence">Thomas Lawrence</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In his lifetime many critics called him a traitor to the Tory cause, or as "a Liberal wolf in sheep's clothing", because his final position reflected liberal ideas.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Others idealised Peel in heroic terms; <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Carlyle" title="Thomas Carlyle">Thomas Carlyle</a> referred to him as a "reforming Hercules" in <i><a href="/wiki/Latter-Day_Pamphlets" title="Latter-Day Pamphlets">Latter-Day Pamphlets</a></i> (1850).<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
The latter would become the consensus view of scholars for much of the 20th century. Historian <a href="/wiki/Boyd_Hilton" title="Boyd Hilton">Boyd Hilton</a> wrote that he was portrayed as:<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The great Conservative patriot: a pragmatic gradualist, as superb in his grasp of fundamental issues as he was adroit in handling administrative detail, intelligent enough to see through abstract theories, a conciliator who put nation before party and established consensus politics.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Biographer <a href="/wiki/Norman_Gash" title="Norman Gash">Norman Gash</a> wrote that Peel "looked first, not to party, but to the state; not to programmes, but to national expediency".<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Gash added that among his personal qualities were, "administrative skill, capacity for work, personal integrity, high standards, a sense of duty [and] an outstanding intellect".<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Gash emphasised the role of personality in Peel's political career:<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>Peel was endowed with great intelligence and integrity, and an immense capacity for hard work. A proud, stubborn, and quick-tempered man he had a passion for creative achievement; and the latter part of his life was dominated by his deep concern for the social condition of the country. Though his great debating and administrative talents secured him an outstanding position in Parliament, his abnormal sensitivity and coldness of manner debarred him from popularity among his political followers, except for the small circle of his intimate friends. As an administrator he was one of the greatest public servants in British history; in politics he was a principal architect of the modern conservative tradition. By insisting on changes unpalatable to many of his party, he helped to preserve the flexibility of the parliamentary system and the survival of aristocratic influence. The repeal of the Corn Laws in 1846 won him immense prestige in the country, and his death in 1850 caused a national demonstration of sorrow unprecedented since the death of William Pitt in 1806.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Peel is featured on the cover of <a href="/wiki/The_Beatles" title="The Beatles">The Beatles</a>' <a href="/wiki/Sgt._Pepper%27s_Lonely_Hearts_Club_Band_(album)" class="mw-redirect" title="Sgt. Pepper&#39;s Lonely Hearts Club Band (album)"><i>Sgt. Pepper's Lonely Hearts Club Band</i></a> album.
</p><p>A 2021 study in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Economic_Journal" title="The Economic Journal">The Economic Journal</a></i> found that the repeal of the corn laws adversely affected the welfare of the top 10% of income earners in Britain, whereas the bottom 90% of income earners gained.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Memorials">Memorials</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Memorials">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Statues">Statues</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Peel&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Statues">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Statues of Sir Robert Peel are found in the following British and Australian locations:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1147244281">.mw-parser-output .div-col{margin-top:0.3em;column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .div-col-small{font-size:90%}.mw-parser-output .div-col-rules{column-rule:1px solid #aaa}.mw-parser-output .div-col dl,.mw-parser-output .div-col ol,.mw-parser-output .div-col ul{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .div-col li,.mw-parser-output .div-col dd{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul{line-height:inherit;list-style:none;margin:0}.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol li,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul li{margin-bottom:0}</style><div class="div-col" style="column-width: 30em;">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Peel_Memorial,_Bury" title="Peel Memorial, Bury">Memorial</a> outside the Robert Peel public house in <a href="/wiki/Bury,_Greater_Manchester" title="Bury, Greater Manchester">Bury</a> town centre, his birthplace.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Parliament_Square" title="Parliament Square">Parliament Square</a>, London.</li>
<li>Peel Park in <a href="/wiki/Accrington" title="Accrington">Accrington</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Winckley_Square" title="Winckley Square">Winckley Square</a> in <a href="/wiki/Preston,_Lancashire" title="Preston, Lancashire">Preston</a> city centre.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/West_Midlands_Police" title="West Midlands Police">West Midlands Police</a> Training Centre, <a href="/wiki/Edgbaston" title="Edgbaston">Edgbaston</a>, Birmingham.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Piccadilly_Gardens" title="Piccadilly Gardens">Piccadilly Gardens</a> in Manchester.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Montrose,_Angus" title="Montrose, Angus">Montrose</a> town centre.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Woodhouse_Moor" title="Woodhouse Moor">Woodhouse Moor</a>, <a href="/wiki/Leeds" title="Leeds">Leeds</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Tamworth,_Staffordshire" title="Tamworth, Staffordshire">Tamworth</a> town centre.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/George_Square" title="George Square">George Square</a>, Glasgow.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Peel_Park,_Bradford" title="Peel Park, Bradford">Peel Park, Bradford</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Wool_Exchange,_Bradford" title="Wool Exchange, Bradford">Wool Exchange, Bradford</a>.</li>
<li>Peel Centre, <a href="/wiki/Hendon_Police_College" title="Hendon Police College">Hendon Police College</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hendon" title="Hendon">Hendon</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Gawsworth_Old_Hall" title="Gawsworth Old Hall">Gawsworth Old Hall</a>, Cheshire.</li>
<li>High Street, <a href="/wiki/Dronfield" title="Dronfield">Dronfield</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Sandy_Bay,_Tasmania" title="Sandy Bay, Tasmania">Sandy Bay, Tasmania</a>, Australia.</li></ul></div>
